\Gamma_{2}
to each of the
q^{2}
non-isotropic points on a tangent. Therefore
\lambda
is the identity on
\Gamma_{2}
, thus the kernel of the projection
\pi
is trivial, and
\pi
is a monomorphism.
∎

Lemma 7.1.2
.

The automorphism group
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
of
NU(3,q^{2})
,
q\neq 2
, acts 2-transitively on tangents lines.

Proof.

Since the action of
P\Gamma U(3,q)
is
2
-transitive on the set of isotropic points of the Hermitian curve, the assertion follows from Lemma
7.0.2
and Lemma
7.1.1
taking into account the fact that every isotropic point is the tangency point of a unique tangent to
H(2,q^{2})
.
∎

Lemma 7.1.3
.

\mathfrak{G}_{2}
does not act
3
-transitively on the set of tangents of
NU(3,q^{2})
.

Proof.

In
PG(2,q^{2})
let us consider the following two different configurations of
3
-tangent lines: three concurrent tangents or three tangents which intersect pairwise in three points
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
on a self-polar triangle. In the graph
NU(3,q^{2})
the former set corresponds to three maximal cliques intersecting in their common vertex, whereas in the latter it corresponds to three maximal cliques intersecting pairwise in three different vertices. It is clear that any automorphism of the graph cannot map the former set to the latter, since they are different adjacence configurations in the graph
NU(3,q^{2})
.
∎

7.2
Proof of Theorem
7.0.1

7.2.1
The case
q>2

From now on, assume that
q>2
. Let
M
be the minimal normal subgroup of
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
(it is unique by the
2
-transitivity of
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
). Observe that
M
cannot be elementary abelian since its order is not a power of a prime (as it contains
P\Gamma U(3,q)
as its subgroup), so
M
must be a simple group. We show that among the finite simple groups which are minimal normal subgroups of
2
-transitive groups, only a few can actually occur in our case. For this purpose we refer to Cameron’s list
[
28
]
reported in Table
7.1
and to
[
51
]
and
[
121
]
for the details about standard group theory arguments. Table
7.1
belong directly to the known Classification of Finite Simple Groups, see
[
121
]
, and consider the theory of finite permutation groups with the assumption that the finite simple groups are known.

which can occur as minimal normal subgroups of

.

The Alternating Group
A_{d}
is ruled out by Lemma
7.1.3
, as it acts
3
-transitively on the sets of tangents.

It is easily seen that
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
is not any of the sporadic groups in Table
7.1
since their degree is different from
q^{3}+1
.

Moreover, for the same reason,
PSL(d,u),~d>2
has also to be excluded by
\frac{u^{d}-1}{u-1}\neq q^{3}+1
. In fact, if
\frac{u^{d}-1}{u-1}=q^{3}+1
were true we would have

u^{d-1}+u^{d-2}+\dots+u=q^{3}.

Observe that
u
and
q
must be powers of the same prime
p
, and this would lead to a contradiction because the right side of the equation is still a power of
p
whereas

u^{d-1}+u^{d-2}+\dots+u=u(u^{d-2}+u^{d-3}+\dots+1)\neq p^{k},

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Finally, when
d=2
, a
PSL(2,u)
is not a subgroup of a
PSU(3,q)
, and this dismiss
PSL(2,8)
.

Before analysing the remaining candidates, take a normal subgroup
M
of
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
.
Then
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
acts on
M
in its natural way:

\begin{array}[]{lccc}\varphi_{g}:&M&\longrightarrow&M\\
&m&\mapsto&m^{g}:=g^{-1}mg,\\
\end{array}

for all
g\in\mathfrak{G}_{2}.

Lemma 7.2.1
.

\varphi_{g}=id\Leftrightarrow g=1

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\varphi_{g}=id\Rightarrow g=1
. From
\varphi_{g}=id
it follows
g\in C_{\mathfrak{G}_{2}}(M)
where
C_{\mathfrak{G}_{2}}(M)
is the centraliser of
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
in
M
. Suppose by contradiction that
C_{\mathfrak{G}_{2}}(M)
is not trivial. Then it must be primitive since
M
is
2
-transitive (see
[
51
, Theorem 4.B]
). Let
t
be a tangent line, and consider
c\in C_{\mathfrak{G}_{2}}(M)
. Then, there exists
\alpha\in\mathfrak{G}_{2}
which fixes only
t
. In our case this implies that
t
is also fixed by
c
, that is,

c(\alpha(t))=c(t),\quad\alpha(c(t))=c(\alpha(t))=c(t).

Therefore, every tangent line is fixed by
c
, and this implies that
c
fixes every point as tangent lines are the maximal cliques, so

C_{\mathfrak{G}_{2}}(M)
is trivial, a contradiction.
∎

Lemma
7.2.1
shows that
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
is a subgroup of
Aut(M)
.

Sz(u),~Ree(u),~PSp(2d,2)
cannot be the minimal normal subgroup of
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
as their automorphism group does not contain
P\Gamma U(3,q)
, we refer the reader to
[
121
]
for all the details.

Our discussion on the groups in Table
7.1
together with lemma
7.2.1
yield that
M=PSU(3,q)
. Since
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
Aut(M)\cong P\Gamma U(3,q)
(see
[
121
, section 3.6.3]
), it follows from Lemma
7.0.2
that
\mathfrak{G}_{2}\cong P\Gamma U(3,q)
.

7.2.2
The case
q=2

In the smallest case
q=2
, consider the Hermitian curve
H(2,4)
with
q^{3}+1=9
isotropic points. The graph
NU(3,4)
has
q^{4}+q^{2}+1-q^{3}+1=12
vertices. Through an external point
P\in PG(2,4)\setminus H(2,4)
there are
q+1=3
tangent lines, and each line is incident with
q^{2}-1=3
non-isotropic points other than
P
. Hence the graph is 9-regular. Now let
P
and
Q
be two non-adjacent vertices, then
PQ
is a
(q+1)
-secant line. Through
P
there are
q+1
tangent lines, and the same number through
Q
, and they meet each other in
(q+1)^{2}
other common neighbours, i.e.
\mu=9
. Now let
P
and
Q
be two adjacent vertices. Then
PQ
is tangent to
H
at
T
. On
PQ
there are
q^{2}-2
non-isotropic points other than
P
and
Q
, so that they have at least
q^{2}-2
common neighbours. Moreover, through
P
there are
q
tangents other than
PQ
, and the same number through
Q
, and they meet each other in
q^{2}
other common neighbours, i.e.
\lambda=6
. The complementary graph
\overline{NU(3,4)}
has parameters
(v^{\prime},k^{\prime},\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})=(12,2,1,0)
. Therefore it is a trivial strongly regular graph with four connected components isomorphic to the complete graph
K_{3}
. Since
Aut(NU(3,4))=Aut(\overline{NU(3,4)})
, it is enough to find the automorphism group of the complementary graph. Observe that
Aut(\overline{NU(3,4)})
is the wreath product of four copies of the automorphism group of
K_{3}
, which is the dihedral group
D_{3}\cong S_{3}
, by the Symmetric group of degree
4
acting as a permutation group on the four connected components:

\mathfrak{G}_{2}=Aut(NU(3,4))\cong S_{3}\wr S_{4}\cong S_{3}^{4}\rtimes S_{4}.

In particular,
|\mathfrak{G}_{2}|=31104.

It should be noted that in the graph
\Gamma_{2}
we make distinction between the two idempotent kinds of maximal cliques, whether we add or do not the fifth point on the Hermitian curve
H(2,4)
, i.e.
\mathfrak{G}_{2}\ncong Aut(\Gamma_{2})\cong P\Gamma U(3,2)
.

Figure 7.1:
NU(3,4)
.

7.3
Non-classical unitals

As said in Section
7.2
, besides the classical one, there are other known unitals in
PG(2,q^{2})
and all the known unitals of
PG(2,q^{2})
arise from a construction due to Buekenhout: a Buekenhout unital of
PG(2,q^{2})
is either an orthogonal Buekenhout-Metz unital or a Buekenhout-Tits unital. recall that an orthogonal Buekenhout-Metz unital of
PG(2,q^{2})
,
q>2
, is projectively equivalent to one of the following form:

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha,\beta}=\{(x,\alpha x^{2}+\beta x^{q+1}+z,1)|z\in F_{q},x\in F_{q^{2}}\}\cup\{(0,1,0)\},

where
\alpha,\beta
are elements in
GF(q^{2})
such that
(\beta-\beta^{q})^{2}+4\alpha^{q+1}
is a non-square in
F_{q}
if
q
is odd, or
\beta\notin F_{q}
and
\frac{\alpha^{q+1}}{(\beta+\beta^{q})^{2}}
has absolute trace
0
if
q
is even and
q>2
, while a Buekenhout-Tits unital of
PG(2,q^{2})
is projectively equivalent to

\mathcal{U}_{T}=\{(x_{0}+x_{1}\beta,(x_{0}^{\delta+2}+x_{0}x_{1}+x_{1}^{\delta})\beta+z,1)|x_{0},x_{1},z\in F\}\cup\{(0,1,0)\},

where
\beta
is an element of
F_{q^{2}}\setminus F_{q}
and
\delta=2^{\frac{m+1}{2}}
. Another class of abstract unitals of order
q=3^{2n+1}
was discovered by H. Lüneburg in
[
85
]
. Let
q=3^{2n+1}
, and
Ree(q)
be the Ree group of order
(q^{3}+1)q^{3}(q-1)
. The group
Ree(q)
has a 2-transitive action on
q^{3}+1
points, by conjugation on the set of all 3-Sylow. The pointwise stabilizer of two points is cyclic of order
q-1
and contains a unique involution.
Ree(q)
has a unique conjugacy class of involutions, and any involution fixes
q+1
points. The blocks of the Ree unital
R(q)
are the sets of fixed points of the involutions of
Ree(q)
. For
q\geq 27
,
R(q)
has no embeddings, while all embeddings of
R(3)
are completely classified, see
[
89
]
for all details. Moreover in
[
2
]
the authors constructed examples of cyclic unitals for
a=4,6
, the latter being the first example of unital in which
a
is not a prime power. Let
E(s)
be the cyclic group
C_{s}
if
s
is prime or the direct product
C_{s_{1}}\times C_{s_{2}}\times\ldots\times C_{s_{n}}
if
s=s_{1}s_{2}\ldots s_{n}
with all
s_{i}
’s primes. Let
p,q
be prime powers such that
p-1
divides
q-1
, then there exists a Steiner system
S(2,p,pq)
which admits
E(pq)
as point-regular automorphism group. Moreover if
p
and
q
are distinct primes, the design is cyclic. Unitals of order 4 and 6 arise respectively from
(p,q)=(5,13)
and
(p,q)=(7,31)
. Finding the automorphism group of non-classical unital is an open, difficult, problem. Theoretical results and computations performed with
GAP

[
65
]
gives us results about the explicit factors of
Aut(\mathcal{U})
for small values of
a
:

\mathcal{U}

a

Aut(\mathcal{U})

Hermitian curve
H(2,q^{2})

2

P\Gamma U(3,2)

Hermitian curve
H(2,q^{2})

q=p^{h}

P\Gamma U(3,q)

Ree unital
R(q)

3

Ree(3)

Buekenhout-Metz orthogonal unital

3

(C_{3}\times C_{3}\times C_{3})\rtimes Q_{8}
,
[
65
]

Buekenhout-Tits unital

3

((C_{4}\times C_{4})\rtimes C_{8})\rtimes C_{6}
,
[
65
]

Bagchi-Bagchi unital

6

C_{7}\rtimes(C_{31}\rtimes C_{30})
,
[
65
]

Table 7.2:
Groups acting as automorphism groups on unitals of order
a
.

Now let
\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}
be the graph whose vertices are the points of the projective plane
\Pi
not in the unital and two vertices are adjacent if the line joining them is tangent to
\mathcal{U}
. From Proposition
6.2.1
, the graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}
is strongly regular with parameters
(q^{2}(q^{2}-q+1),(q+1)(q^{2}-1),2(q^{2}-1),(q+1)^{2})
. If the unital is not embeddable, we need the definition of dual unital defined on the dual projective plane, where the
q^{3}+1
tangents are considered as points, and the blocks are the pencil of tangent lines from an external point. In the dual unital, we have a one-to-one correspondance between points of the unital and tangents, and between points in
PG(2,q^{2})\setminus H(2,q^{2})
and secants, given by the unitary polarity. The vertex-set of the dual graph is now the set of all secants, i.e. blocks of the unital. As in the tangent graph two vertices are adjacent if the points lie on the same tangent, in the dual
block graphs
two vertices are adjacent if the secants intersect in an isotropic point, i.e. adjacency between two blocks is equivalent to the intersection in the unital.

Proposition 7.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an unital of order
a
(classical or not, embeddable or not). The block graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}
is strongly regular with parameters
(a^{2}(a^{2}-a+1),(a+1)(a^{2}-1),2(a^{2}-1),(a+1)^{2})
.

7.4
EKR Theorems in unitals

In their seminal paper
[
60
]
, the authors proved the following theorem.

Theorem 7.4.1
.

[
60
]

Choose integers
k
and
n
such that
0<2k\leq n
, let
\mathcal{F}
be a family of subsets
of size
k
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that for each
F,F^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
,
F\cup F^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
. Then

\mathcal{F}\leq\binom{n-1}{k-1}.

Moreover, if
2k<n
, equality holds if and only if
\mathcal{F}
is the family of all
k
-sets through a fixed element
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
.

Consider in the set of
a^{3}+1
points of
\mathcal{U}
, the family of
(a+1)
-subsets defined by the blocks. A maximal clique in the block graph is equivalent to an EKR-intersecting family.

Theorem 7.4.2
.

[
46
, Theorem 6.4]

Let
\mathcal{U}
be a unital of order
a
and let
S
be a maximal Erdős-Ko-Rado set on
\mathcal{U}
.

1.

If
a\geq 5
then either
|S|=a^{2}
and
S
is a point-pencil, or else
|S|\leq a^{2}-a+a^{\frac{2}{3}}-\frac{2}{3}a^{\frac{1}{3}}+1
.

2.

If
a=4
then either
|S|=16=a^{2}
and
S
is a point-pencil, or else
|S|\leq 13=a^{2}-a+1
.

3.

If
a=3
then either
|S|=9=a^{2}
and
S
is a point-pencil, or else
|S|\leq 8
.

Note that in the dual representation, the point pencil of
q^{2}
blocks arises from
q^{2}
points on a same tangent. Therefore, Theorem
7.4.2
is a generalization of Lemma
7.0.3
. As in Lemma
7.0.3
, from this result we get the needed informations on the automorphisms.

Theorem 7.4.3
.

Let
\mathcal{U}
be an unital of order
a>2
. The automorphism group of the block graph
\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}
is isomorphic to the automorphism group of
\mathcal{U}
.

Proof.

Since the structure of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}}
belongs to the intersections in the unital, each
\lambda\in Aut(\mathcal{U})
stabilizes the graph. To see the converse, consider
\mu\in Aut(\Gamma_{\mathcal{U}})
. The action of
\mu
permutes all maximal cliques of size
a^{2}
, preserving incidences in both the graph and the design. From Theorem
7.4.2
, since
a>2
, maximal cliques of size
a^{2}
are only the
canonical cliques
, i.e. point-pencils. Since points and canonical cliques are in one-to-one correspondance, the induced action of
\mu
on the unital is described considering the composition
\mu
with the bijection
\pi
between points and point-pencils of blocks.
1
1

1

The proof of this theorem was found with S. Adriaensen, J. De Beule and F. Romaniello, that we are very grateful to.

∎

Appendix A
Axioms of polar spaces

F. Veldkamp in
[
118
,
119
]
introduced the following axioms of polar spaces. A space
S
is called polar if

I

\forall x,y\in S
,
x\leq y
and
y\leq x\iff x=y
.

II

\forall x,y,z\in S
, if
x\leq y
and
y\leq z
, then
x\leq z
.

III

Every nonempty set of elements of
S
has an intersection.

IV

For every element
a\in S
, the set of all
x\leq a
is a projective space of finite rank.

V

Every element of
S
is contained in some maximal element, all maximal elements have the same (finite) rank which is called the index of
S
.

VI

If
x
and
y
are elements of S with intersection 0, then there exist maximal elements
a
and
b
in
S
with intersection 0 and such that
x\leq a
,
y\leq b
.

VI

Let
a
and
b
be maximal with intersection 0. For every
x\leq a
there exists one and only one
x^{\delta}\leq b
such that
x\cup x^{\delta}
is maximal.

VIII

There exists a finite number of points such that
S
is the only flat subset of
S
that contains all those points.

IX

If
u
and
v
are maximal elements of
S
and are considered as projective spaces as in IV, then there exists a projectivity of
u
upon
v
.

X

If
a\in S
, then the projective geometry
x\leq a
admits the
Desargues configuration
, see Figure
A.1
.

Figure A.1:
Desargues Configuration.

Later the axioms were simplified by J. Tits in
[
111
, Chapters 7-9]
as follows. Let start with a set
S
in which some subsets are called
subspaces
. There is an integer
n\geq 1
called the rank of
S
.
S
must respect the four following axioms P1)-P4).

P1)

A subspace
L
, together with the subspaces it contains, is a
d
-dimensional projective space,
-1\leq d\leq n-1
.

P2)

The intersection of two subspaces is a subspace.

P3)

Given a subspace
L
of dimension
n-1
and a point
p\in S\setminus L
, there exists a unique subspace
M
containing
p
and such that
M\cap L
is of dimension
n-2
and it contains all points of
L
collinear with
p
.

P4)

There exist two disjoint subspaces of dimension
n-1
.

From the point of view of incidence geometry, axioms of polar spaces were also given by F. Buekenhout and E. Shult in
[
26
]
in 1974. In their approach a polar space is an incidence structure of points and lines
(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{L},I)
with
I\subseteq\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{L}
such that

1.

Any line contains at least three points.

2.

No point is incident with all others.

3.

Any chain of singular subspaces is of finite length.

4.

Let
x\in\mathcal{P}
, and
\ell\in\mathcal{L}
, with
x\not\in\ell
, then
x
is collinear with one or all points of
\ell
.

For more details on history of polar spaces, we refer the reader to
[
25
]
.

Appendix B
Maximal curves over finite fields

We give some definitions and results about
F_{q}
-maximal curves used in Chapter
2
. For details and proofs; see
[
71
]
. By an
algebraic curve
defined over
F_{q}
we mean a projective, geometrically irreducible, non-singular algebraic curve
\mathcal{X}
of
PG(r,q)
viewed as a curve of
PG(r,\overline{F_{q}})
where
\overline{F_{q}}
is the algebraic closure of
F_{q}
. Each curve
\mathcal{X}
is birationally isomorphic to a plane curve
\mathcal{X}
with only ordinary singularities.

Let
K
be an algebraic closed field, consider a polynomial in two indeterminate
P\in K[X,Y]
of polynomial degree
d
. We associate to
P
the homogeneous polynomial
P^{*}\in K[X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}]
where
X=\frac{X_{1}}{X_{0}}
,
Y=\frac{X_{2}}{X_{0}}
and
P^{*}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2})=X_{0}^{d}P(\frac{X_{1}}{X_{0}},\frac{X_{2}}{X_{0}})
.

Definition B.1
.

The
algebraic plane projective curve
of
affine equation

P(X,Y)=0
, or
homogeneous equation

P^{*}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2})=0
, is the variety

\mathcal{X}=v(P)=v(P^{*})=\{(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})\in PG(2,K)|P^{*}(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2})=0\}.

The
degree
of
\mathcal{X}
is the polynomial degree of
P
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
a curve defined over the algebraic closure of a finite field
\overline{F_{q}}
. Points of
\mathcal{X}
lying on
F_{q}
are usually called
F_{q}
-rational points
.

Definition B.2
.

[
112
, Proposition 17]

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an absolutely irreducible plane curve of degree
d
with only ordinary singularities, if
P_{1},\ldots,P_{k}
are the singular points of
\mathcal{C}
and they have multiplicities
r_{1},\ldots,r_{k}
, then the genus
\mathfrak{g}
of
\mathcal{C}
is given by

\mathfrak{g}=\frac{1}{2}(n-1)(n-2)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}(r_{i}-1).

(B.1)

The genus of a curve
\mathcal{X}
is defined by the genus of any plane curve
\mathcal{C}
birationally isomorphic to
\mathcal{X}
.

Theorem B.0.1

(
Hasse-Weil bound
)
.

Consider an algebraic curve
\mathcal{X}
defined over
F_{q}
, and let
N_{q}(\mathcal{X})
be the set of its
F_{q}
-rational points. Then

|N_{q}(\mathcal{X})-(q+1)|\leq 2\mathfrak{g}\sqrt{q}.

(B.2)

An algebraic curve
\mathcal{X}
defined over
F_{q}
is
F_{q}
-maximal
if the number of its
F_{q}
-rational points attains the Hasse-Weil upper bound.
By the
Natural Embedding Theorem
[
80
]
, each
F_{q^{2}}
-maximal curve is
F_{q^{2}}
-isomorphic to a degree
q+1
curve lying in the Hermitian variety of
PG(r,q^{2})
where
r
is bounded by the Frobenius dimension
d
of
\mathcal{X}
. Here, the
d
is defined to be the dimension of a divisor
|(q+1)P_{0}|
where
P_{0}
is any
F_{q^{2}}
-rational point of
\mathcal{X}
.

Theorem B.0.2

(
Natural Embedding Theorem
)
.

Every
F_{q^{2}}
maximal curve
\mathcal{X}
of genus
g\geq 0
is isomorphic over
F_{q^{2}}
to a curve of
PG(m,K)
of degree
q+1
lying
on a non-degenerate Hermitian variety defined over
F_{q^{2}}
.

Appendix C
[n,k]_{q}
-linear codes and Hamming distance

In this Appendix we list first properties of linear codes, see for more details
[
117
]
.

Definition C.1
.

An
[n,k]_{q}
-
linear code

\mathcal{C}
is a subspace of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
of dimension
k
. The elements of
\mathcal{C}
are said
codewords
.

Definition C.2
.

•

The
Hamming distance
between two codewords
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
is the number of entries in which
x
and
y
differ:
d(x,y)=|\{i|x_{i}\neq y_{i}\}|
.

•

The
minimum (Hamming) distance
of a code
\mathcal{C}
is

d=d(\mathcal{C})=min\{d(x,y)|x,y\in\mathcal{C},x\neq y\}.

In this case we say
\mathcal{C}
is a
[n,k,d]_{q}
-linear code.

Proposition C.0.1
.

The Hamming distance is a metric, i.e.
\forall x,y,z\in\mathcal{C}
:

1.

d(x,y)\geq 0
and equality holds if and only if
x=y
;

2.

d(x,y)=d(y,x)
;

3.

d(x,y)+d(y,z)\geq d(x,z)
.

Proof.

1 and 2 are quite trivial, we focus on 3. If
d(x,z)=0
,
x=z
and we have done since
2d(x,y)\geq d(x,x)=0
. If
x\neq z
, say w.l.o.g.
x_{j}\neq z_{j}
for a fixed
1\leq j\leq n
. Then it must occur at least one condition between
x_{j}\neq y_{j}
and
y_{j}\neq z_{j}
. If we repeat the argument for all entries
i
in which
x_{i}\neq z_{i}
we get the thesis.
∎

Theorem C.0.2
.

•

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
[n,k,d]_{q}
-linear code. Then,
\mathcal{C}
can correct
\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
errors.

•

If is used for detection,
\mathcal{C}
can detect
d-1
errors.

Proof.

•

Consider balls centered in a codeword of radius
\lfloor\frac{d-1}{2}\rfloor
. Since the minimum distance is
d
, we have the thesis, considering the metric properties of Hamming distance.

•

Trivial, since the minimum distance is
d
,
d-1
errors cannot send one codeword in another one.

∎

Definition C.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
[n,k]_{q}
-linear code.

•

The
Hamming weight
of a codeword
c
is the number of non-zero entries of
c
, i.e.
w(c)=d(c,0)
.

•

The
minimum weight
of a code
\mathcal{C}
is
w(\mathcal{C})=min\{w(c)|c\in\mathcal{C},c\neq 0\}
.

The following proposition allows us to study the minimum distance of a code studying weights of codewords.

Proposition C.0.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a
[n,k]_{q}
-linear code, then
d(\mathcal{C})=w(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Since
d(x,y)=w(x-y)
and the linear subspaces are closed under addition we have

d(\mathcal{C})=min\{d(x,y)|x,y\in\mathcal{C},x\neq y\}=min\{w(c)|c\in\mathcal{C},c\neq 0\}=w(\mathcal{C}).

∎

Definition C.4
.

The
weight distribution
of a
[n,k]_{q}
-code
\mathcal{C}
is the
(n+1)
-tuple
(1=W_{\mathcal{C}}(0),W_{\mathcal{C}}(1),\ldots,W_{\mathcal{C}}(n))
.

Definition C.5
.

The
weight enumerator
of a
[n,k]_{q}
-code
\mathcal{C}
is the polynomial

W_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)=\sum_{i=0}^{n}W_{\mathcal{C}}(i)X^{n-i}Y^{i}=X^{n}+W_{\mathcal{C}}(1)X^{n-1}Y+\ldots+W_{\mathcal{C}}(n)Y^{n}.

Definition C.6
.

A
two-weight code
is an
[n,k,d]
-linear code
C
such that
|\{W|\exists v\in C\setminus\{0\}\hskip 5.69054pt,w(v)=W\}|=2
, i.e. the weight distribution have
3
non-zero entries and the weight enumerator have
3
non-zero coefficients.

Appendix D
Combinatorial designs

In this section we report some basic notions from design theory. The approach is same as in
[
86
, Chapter 6]
. For more details on combinatorial designs; see
[
30
]
.

Definition D.1
.

For a finite set
\mathcal{P}
with
v
elements, called
points
, together with a family
\mathcal{B}
of
b
nonempty subsets of
\mathcal{P}
of size
k
, called
blocks
, the pair
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B})
is called
t-(v,k,\lambda)

design
, or
t
-
design
, or also
design
, if each subset of
\mathcal{P}
of size
t
is contained in exactly
\lambda
blocks. In case
v=b
, it is called a
symmetric design
.

Definition D.2
.

An
automorphism
of the design
\mathcal{D}
is a permutation on points which induces a permutation on
\mathcal{B}
.

Proposition D.0.1
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{B})
be a
t-(v,k,\lambda)
design. For each integer
1\geq i\geq t
, let

\lambda_{i}=\lambda\frac{\binom{v-i}{t-i}}{\binom{k-i}{t-i}}.

Then
\mathcal{D}
be a
i-(v,k,\lambda_{i})
design for each such
i
.

Proof.

An equivalent reformulation of the claim is that through each
i
-subset of
\mathcal{P}
there pass exactly
\lambda_{i}
blocks. Fix an
i
-subset
X
, and count twice the pairs
(A,B)
when
A\subset\mathcal{P}\setminus X
,
|A|=t-i
and
B
is a block containing
A\cup X
. For a fixed block
B
through the
\lambda_{i}
blocks containing
X
, there are exactly
\binom{k-i}{t-i}
way to choose the
t-i
points of
A
in
B\setminus X
, so that the number of pairs
(A,B)
equals
\nu=\lambda_{i}\binom{k-i}{t-i}
. On the other hand we have
\binom{v-i}{t-i}
choices for
A
. Since
|A\cup X|=t
,
B
may be chosen in
\lambda
ways, since
\mathcal{D}
is a
t-(v,k,\lambda)
design. So
\nu=\lambda\binom{v-i}{t-i}=\lambda_{i}\binom{k-i}{t-i}
, and this proves the claim.
∎

A corollary of Proposition
D.0.1
is that the number of blocks equals
b=\lambda_{0}=\lambda\frac{\binom{v}{t}}{\binom{k}{t}}
. Moreover through each point there pass exactly
r=\lambda_{1}=\lambda\frac{\binom{v-1}{t-1}}{\binom{k-1}{t-1}}
blocks. Also,
\lambda_{t}=\lambda
. Therefore, any
t-(v,k,\lambda)
design is a
tactical configuration
, i.e. an incidence structure with
v
points and
b
blocks, with
k
points on each block and
r
blocks through each point. A straightforward double counting argument gives the following identity for tactical configurations.

Corollary D.0.2

(
Tactical configuration
)
.

vr=bk.

Example D.3
.

Points and
d
-subspaces of
PG(n,q)
forms a 2-design with
b=\begin{bmatrix}n+1\\
d+1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{q}
, whose parameters are

v=\begin{bmatrix}n+1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{q}=q^{n}+q^{n-1}+\ldots+1=\theta_{n},

k=\begin{bmatrix}d+1\\
1\\
\end{bmatrix}_{q}=\theta_{d},

\lambda=\begin{cases}\begin{bmatrix}n-2\\
d-2\\
\end{bmatrix}_{q}=\frac{(q^{n-1}-1)(q^{n-2}-1)\ldots(q^{n-d+1}-1)}{(q^{d-1}-1)(q^{d-2}-1)\ldots(q-1)}&\mbox{ if }d>1,\\
\hskip 56.9055pt1&\mbox{ if }d=1.\end{cases}

The design is denoted by
PG_{d}(n,q)
, and by
PG(n,q)
for
d=1
. Its automorphism group is
P\Gamma L(n+1,q)
for all values of
d
.
PG_{d}(n,q)
is symmetric if and only if
d=n-1
.

Example D.4
.

In the previous construction, the Fano plane
PG(2,2)
, see Figure
1.1
, is a
2-(7,3,1)
design.

Definition D.5
.

Let
a
be a positive integer, a
2-(a^{3}+1,a+1,1)
design is called
unital
.

Proposition D.0.3
.

Points and intersections with secants of a Hermitian curve
H(2,q^{2})
define an unital.

Proof.

H(2,q^{2})
has
q^{3}+1
points, and the secant lines meet the curve in
q+1
points. Since lines of
PG(2,q^{2})
are either tangent or secant to
H(2,q^{2})
, through a pair of isotropic points there pass exactly one secant.
∎
